It all started after this really intense late-night confrontation I had with my regional boss. Ever since that night, it's like I can't turn it off. When I'm in the office now, I just keep checking where my boss is. I'm always looking at where the exits are, or... or checking to see if anyone is coming up behind me. No nightmares about it, and I don't feel like I'm reliving it or having flashbacks. It's really just the being on edge while I'm at work that's the problem. When I go home and go to sleep, I'm fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding your regional boss or certain work situations since the confrontation?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still have to do my job, so I can't really avoid him or the office. I still go to all the meetings and everything. I just... I'm just really tense when I'm there, you know? Yeah, basically ever since that late-night confrontation with him happened, it's just been going on like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just this feeling of being on guard all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes and included symptoms like palpitations, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I mean... my heart definitely races when I get startled by a loud noise, like I mentioned, but it doesn't just happen out of nowhere. And I haven't had any sweating or trouble breathing, or that intense fear you're describing. It's more just a constant feeling of being on edge, rather than a sudden episode like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't have any thoughts that I feel like I *have* to think about, or anything I feel forced to do over and over. It's really just that constant feeling of being on guard that I mentioned, but nothing like what you're asking about.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been causing you a lot of stress lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... well, this has been going on for about two weeks now. It all started after this really intense late-night confrontation I had with my regional boss. Since then, I've just been dealing with all this stress and feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Did the confrontation with your regional boss trigger these feelings of being on guard and startling easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Ever since that night, it's like I can't turn it off. When I'm in the office now, I just keep checking where my boss is. I'm always looking at where the exits are, or... or checking to see if anyone is coming up behind me. It's really distracting, honestly, because I'm supposed to be focused on my team, but I'm just constantly watching my back instead.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any nightmares or flashbacks related to the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. No nightmares about it, and I don't feel like I'm reliving it or having flashbacks. It's really just the being on edge while I'm at work that's the problem. When I go home and go to sleep, I'm fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding your regional boss or certain work situations since the confrontation?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still have to do my job, so I can't really avoid him or the office. I still go to all the meetings and everything. I just... I'm just really tense when I'm there, you know? But I'm not staying away or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: It's been about two weeks now. Yeah, basically ever since that late-night confrontation with him happened, it's just been going on like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: No, my sleep's been okay, actually. Like I said before, once I get home and actually get into bed, I'm fine. I just... I fall asleep normally and sleep through the night. It's really just the daytime stuff at the office that's bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling depressed or irritable, since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm depressed or anything. Maybe a little more on edge when I'm at work, but I haven't been snapping at people or feeling really down. It's just that tension I mentioned before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the confrontation with your regional boss is stronger than you would have expected from yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe? I mean, I've had disagreements at work before, but this one just... it really got to me. I guess I didn't expect to still be feeling this tense about it two weeks later. So yeah, maybe it is a bit stronger than I'd normally react.\n\nDoctor: Has your being on edge and constant vigilance at work affected your relationships with your colleagues or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still making it in every day, but... yeah, it's definitely affecting my work. I'm just a lot slower with my accounts and making staffing decisions. I've actually had to ask my team to double-check a few things that I would normally just handle on my own without a second thought. It's frustrating. And with performance reviews coming up soon, I'm honestly pretty worried that these mistakes and the slowdown could end up affecting my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks at work that you've been avoiding since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding anything. I mean, I still have to get my work done, so I'm not skipping meetings or putting off certain tasks or anything like that. It's more just... doing the exact same stuff I always do, but just feeling a lot more tense and taking longer to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the confrontation with your boss would be considered a normal response to a stressful event, or does it feel excessive to you?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know what a normal response is supposed to look like. It was a really stressful situation, so of course I was going to be upset. But I guess feeling this keyed up for this long... maybe it is a bit much. I just know it feels awful, whether it's normal or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least six months in your life where you felt consistently anxious or worried about everyday things, even when there wasn't any particular stressor happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I get stressed out sometimes, sure, but never for six months straight about just everyday stuff. Usually, if I'm worried, there's a clear reason for it, like a big project at work or something. It's never just out of nowhere for that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I'm always looking at where the exits are, or... or checking to see if anyone is coming up behind me.
